| **[DEAF: A Benchmark for Diagnostic Evaluation of Acoustic Faithfulness in Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18048v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Audio Multimodal Large Language Models (Audio MLLMs) demonstrate impressive performance on speech benchmarks, yet it remains unclear whether these models genuinely process acoustic signals or rely on text-based semantic inference. To systematically study this question, we introduce DEAF (Diagnostic Evaluation of Acoustic Faithfulness), a benchmark of over 2,700 conflict stimuli spanning three acoustic dimensions: emotional prosody, background sounds, and speaker identity. Then, we design a controlled multi-level evaluation framework that progressively increases textual influence, ranging from semantic conflicts in the content to misleading prompts and their combination, allowing us to disentangle content-driven bias from prompt-induced sycophancy. We further introduce diagnostic metrics to quantify model reliance on textual cues over acoustic signals. Our evaluation of seven Audio MLLMs reveals a consistent pattern of text dominance: models are sensitive to acoustic variations, yet predictions are predominantly driven by textual inputs, revealing a gap between high performance on standard speech benchmarks and genuine acoustic understanding.</p></details> | 14 pages,6 figures |
| **[ResidencyRL: Reinforcement Learning in Simulated Clinical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07418v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In medical education, physicians convert academic knowledge into clinical expertise through residency: years of training across thousands of encounters, with diverse sources of feedback and progressively greater autonomy. Much of clinical reasoning relies on the patient encounter, a dialogue in which a clinician elicits history, refines diagnostic hypotheses, and decides management under uncertainty. While large language models (LLMs) excel on static medical benchmarks, methods to optimize the full sequence of clinical decisions remain underdeveloped. We present ResidencyRL, a reinforcement learning (RL) method for training clinical artificial intelligence (AI) agents through simulated multi-turn clinical encounters (up to 60 dialogue turns and 8 tool calls per trajectory). ResidencyRL pairs the policy agent with LLM simulators capable of complex, adversarial behaviors, training against a structured reward aligned to diagnostic accuracy, management quality, communication, documentation, and safety. On held-out evaluations, the ResidencyRL agent improves diagnostic accuracy by 7.0% under adversarial conditions (88.0% vs. 81.0%) and reduces missed red flag rates by 31%, demonstrating rigorous mitigation of premature closure. Blinded expert clinicians validated these gains, preferring the trained agent in 87.6% of side-by-side comparisons. The procedural competencies transfer to unseen benchmarks: the agent outperforms the base model across all six clinical axes of the AMIE multi-visit benchmark, and shows consistent directional improvements on AgentClinic and CRAFT-MD. Our findings demonstrate that sequential clinical decision-making can be effectively learned through multi-turn RL in simulation, yielding robust, generalizable capabilities, paving the way towards clinical mastery. Prospective validation with real-world workflows remains necessary to establish clinical utility.</p></details> | Update references |
| **[Task Context Shapes Component Roles in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13895v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models reuse the same attention heads across tasks, but identifying these shared heads does not explain what they do differently in each task. A natural source of inspiration is task-dependent interactions among shared brain regions, which motivate a network view of language-model computation. Here, we compare fixed model components across tasks using identical graph facts, statements specifying which directed connections are present. We measure how interventions affect attention heads and the activation state in later layers (influence), as well as the answer (importance). Task changes redistribute the influence in five open-weight models spanning four families and 1.5-14 billion parameters. In Gemma-2-9B and Qwen3-14B, an activation edit changes an early internal activation vector and switches which fact the model reads while the written instruction stays fixed. The same heads then respond to different graph facts and influence later components differently. For each prompt, we construct two linear response maps, one before the edit and one after it. Each map predicts a later head's response from a measured change in an earlier head. For a separate factual intervention, the map for the corresponding condition reduces mean squared prediction error by 53.6% in Gemma and 5.0% in Qwen compared with the average of the two maps. These findings show that task context changes both which graph facts the shared components respond to and how their outputs affect later computation. A network account may help explain how models use shared parameters to perform different computations. Measurements of task-dependent influence could also guide interventions intended to alter one behavior while preserving others.</p></details> | 9 pages, 5 Figs |
| **[The Illusion of AI Expertise Under Uncertainty: A Probabilistic Paradigm to Interpret Results Under Ground Truth Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05500v6)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmarking the capabilities of AI systems, including Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Models, typically ignores the impact of uncertainty in the underlying ground truth answers from experts. This ambiguity is not just limited to human preferences, but is also consequential even in safety critical domains such as medicine where uncertainty is pervasive. In this paper, we introduce a probabilistic paradigm to theoretically explain how high certainty in ground truth answers is almost always necessary for even an expert to achieve high scores. In contrast, in datasets with high variation in ground truth answers, the expert performance drops substantially resulting in almost no difference between a random labeller and an expert. This characteristic also manifests when comparing models, where uncertainty obfuscates differences between poor and high performing models. Therefore, ignoring uncertainty in ground truth evaluation data can result in the misleading conclusion that a non-expert has similar performance to that of an expert. Given ground truth answer variability and commonly used F1 and accuracy metrics, we bring forth a simple and explainable probabilistic coin toss model to estimate expected accuracyand expected F1 and compare the estimated scores with those that experts (human or model) achieve across multiple domains -- including radiology and models such as GPT-5 and Gemini-3. The results lead to the recommendation that stratification by the probability of the ground truth answer becomes critical when expert performance is relatively low. Under stratified evaluation, performance comparison becomes more reliable in high certainty bins, mitigating the effect of the key confounding factor --- uncertainty.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Query, Different Answer: Run-to-Run Variance in Semantic Query Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13823v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Semantic query engines ask a large language model (LLM) to judge individual rows, and benchmarks compare these engines on the quality of a few executions at temperature 0. We measure how much another execution changes. We re-execute the text and tabular workloads of SemBench, LRO-Bench, and UDA-Bench through their own harnesses, log every one of 1.2 million model calls, and reanalyze SemBench's published executions. At temperature 0, between 0.07% and 10.2% of rows change their operator output over five executions, depending on the model, and up to 24.0% on one task. Plans amplify these changes exponentially in the number of rows an answer exposes, as a possible-worlds model predicts: on UDA-Bench, a few percent of extracted values change, but about two thirds of the answers do. In SemBench's published results, 21% of text results change quality between executions, including on engines whose plans are deterministic. Several per-query system comparisons, and 16 of 28 tests of LRO-Bench's implementation recommendations, are not settled by the executions the benchmarks run. Changing rows are rows on which models disagree with each other; identical requests within one execution disagree as often as across executions; and one execution's decision margin or reasoning length predicts which rows change. Voting reduces the variance but does not remove it, and a calibrated cascade keeps its guarantee while the rows it returns change. We derive reporting rules for benchmarks and design rules for engines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data: https://github.com/semviews/sqp-variance-study</p></details> |
| **[QTestBench: A Test Framework for Evaluating LLM-Generated Quantum Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13811v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong results in classical code generation and are increasingly used to write the unit tests that safeguard that code. Whether they can do the same for quantum software is less clear, because quantum programs are probabilistic, depend on numerical tolerances, and are judged by domain-specific checkers, so a well-formed test can still disagree with the benchmark's verdict. Existing quantum benchmarks measure whether a model produces a correct solution, but not whether its tests can distinguish correct implementations from faulty ones. We introduce QTestBench, a framework that evaluates LLM-generated quantum test suites against independent benchmark checkers, never treating the tests themselves as ground truth. For each task, QTestBench prompts models to produce syntactic, semantic, and behavioral tests, injects candidate solutions at runtime, and compares each test verdict with the checker's label under three validation cohorts, each answering a distinct diagnostic question. We benchmark five LLMs on eight PennyLane QHack challenges and ten Qiskit HumanEval tasks. Generated suites almost always execute, but discrimination lags behind: the best generator reaches a test quality of 0.71 on QHack and 0.34 on Qiskit HumanEval, so executability alone overstates test quality. Oracle-verified synthetic mutants stabilize bug-detection estimates when natural faulty solutions are scarce, as on three of ten Qiskit tasks that yield none. QTestBench offers a reproducible, cross-framework benchmark for the trustworthiness of LLM-generated quantum tests.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 2026 IEEE 2nd International Conference on Quantum Artificial Intelligence (QAI), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Tracing and Taming Hallucinated Terms via Subsequence-Association Filtering and Breadth-Aware Selection for LLM Query Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13810v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are now a standard source of query expansion terms but they hallucinate. They inject non existent entities and off-topic terms that add ranking noise and can hurt strong retrievers. Existing remedies such as corpus grounding, mutual verification and preference alignment treat hallucinated terms as an undifferentiated nuisance to be filtered by agreement heuristics, without a mechanistic account of why a given term appears or a principled criterion for how many terms to keep. We propose Subsequence-Association Filtered, breadth-aware query expansion (SAFE-QE), a training-free, retriever agnostic method that draws on the subsequence-association account of hallucination. We score each candidate expansion term by its corpus conditioned cross-context reproducibility, terms that reappear under randomised query contexts yet lack support in the pseudo-relevant set are flagged as products of dominant hallucinatory associations and down-weighted. Drawing on the characterisation of language generation as a trade-off between consistency (no hallucination) and breadth (coverage), we cast term selection as constrained coverage maximisation over the pseudo-relevant set, keeping a set that is approximately broad but consistent and stable across resampling. We also introduce the Hallucinated Expansion Term Rate, a query side faithfulness metric that counts injected expansion terms no relevant document supports. We evaluate our approach on benchmarked TREC and BEIR collections. Our SAFE-QE is statistically indistinguishable from generative and corpus-steered expansion on nDCG@10 while injecting fewer unsupported terms 4.3 per query against 27.8 for Query2doc and 57.5 for HyDE and on a strong learned sparse retriever, where every expansion method regresses. Faithfulness is therefore available at no measurable cost in ranking quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[Access Paths for Efficient Ordering with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00303v5)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this work, we present the \texttt{LLM ORDER BY} semantic operator as a logical abstraction and conduct a systematic study of its physical implementations. First, we propose several improvements to existing semantic sorting algorithms and introduce a semantic-aware external merge sort algorithm. Our extensive evaluation reveals that no single implementation offers universal optimality on all datasets. From our evaluations, we observe a general scaling relationship between sorting cost and the ordering quality for comparison-based algorithms. Building on these insights, we design a budget-aware optimizer that utilizes heuristic rules, LLM-as-Judge evaluation, and consensus aggregation to dynamically select the near-optimal access path for LLM ORDER BY. In our extensive evaluations, our optimizer consistently achieves ranking accuracy on par with or superior to the best static methods across all benchmarks. We believe that this work provides foundational insights into the principled optimization of semantic operators essential for building robust, large-scale LLM-powered analytic systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[OpenSocialJax: Evaluating Social Adaptation in Sequential Social Dilemmas with Open-Ended Environmental Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13710v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential social dilemma benchmarks capture diverse social interactions, but rarely test whether agents can infer hidden environmental rules through repeated interaction while adapting to other agents. We introduce \textbf{OpenSocialJax}, which extends Clean Up and Commons Harvest across an open-ended distribution of latent environment configurations, systematically varying spatial layouts and hidden rules governing tool crafting, payoffs, and resource dynamics. Each episode samples one configuration from tens of millions of possibilities and holds it fixed across five trials. We evaluate eight large language models, including three closed source models and five open source models. Our evaluation tests whether models can discover hidden rules and adapt to other agents in homogeneous and mixed teams. We find that rule discovery often improves performance but does not reliably sustain cooperation, as the cleaning burden becomes concentrated among fewer agents in OpenCleanup and every team eventually exhausts the shared resource in OpenHarvest. In our mixed arena, one cooperative agent was able to sustain the public good in OpenCleanup at a cost to its own return, but could not prevent resource depletion in OpenHarvest. Together, these results highlight the difficulty of adapting jointly to unfamiliar environmental dynamics and the changing behavior of other agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, including appendix</p></details> |
| **[Inject a Turn, Not a Token: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning. For redirection, we find that under a fixed generation budget, the tested inline injections fail to reliably redirect reasoning, whereas presenting the same instruction in a new user turn changes the model's course. The resulting correction framework, UPTURN (User-Prompt TURN injection at a learned position), requires no LLM fine-tuning: it retains a selected prefix, injects an instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix. | **[A Multi-Agent LLM System for Automated Tuning of Specialized Neural Network Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13663v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many machine learning approaches are two-level: an expert selects a class of methods, within this class the choice of key components is critical and usually manual. This paper addresses the automatic tuning of these components and proposes a framework transferable across models of different nature. The multi-agent large language model (LLM) framework has three roles---analyst, coder, and recorder~---with an experiment log and a sandboxed execution environment. Unlike systems that optimize the entire ML script or search architectures in a discrete space, the framework operates over an open but semantically narrow space: the agent generates code only for the specified components, leaving the overall network structure unchanged. The approach is validated in two directions: tuning a classification spiking neural network with correlation learning, and solving high-order differential equations with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN). Across five tasks---spiking classification on MNIST and Digits, and boundary-value problems for the generalized nonlinear Schrödinger equation (GNLSE) and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky (KS) equations---the agent outperforms the baselines: classification accuracy is 13 percentage points (MNIST) and 6 percentage points (Digits) higher; the error against the analytical single-soliton solution is reduced approximately 2491-fold; the invariant-preservation error drops approximately 325-fold in the selected GNLSE run with a Gaussian perturbation and by more than 100-fold on average over repeated initializations; and the mass drift drops 14-fold for the KS problem with a Gaussian perturbation. The framework thus tunes key components on tasks of different nature without changing its internal structure.</p></details> |  |
| **[ToFu: Exploiting and Fuzzing the LLM-Orchestrator-Tool Trust Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13651v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that interact with external software through tools, with an orchestration layer translating model outputs into structured tool invocations. Evaluates 273 open-source MCP servers (3405 tools); 253 verified semantic security bugs in 118 servers. | **[SPARQL-LLM in the TEXT2SPARQL 2026 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13648v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Querying distributed data represented as Knowledge Graphs (KGs) via SPARQL remains a difficult task, requiring fluency in query syntax, endpoint specifications, and schema details. In this paper, we describe SPARQL-LLM, our open-source KGQA system, in the context of the TEXT2SPARQL 2026 challenge. | **[Humanize Olympiad Agents: A Multi-Session Workflow for Olympiad Problem Solving with Solve, Review, Wiki, Verify](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13632v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are rapidly improving on long-horizon scientific and olympiad tasks. More broadly, a single agent session remains brittle on difficult problems: early errors can propagate, missing domain knowledge can stall progress, and apparent success can mask a misinterpretation or weakening of the original task. We introduce HOA (Humanize Olympiads Agent), an open, flow-level framework that coordinates multiple agent sessions through three complementary feedback channels. First, an independent reviewer evaluates each candidate and provides actionable feedback. Second, a structured wiki supplies task-specific knowledge that may be missing or unreliable in model pretraining. Third, a verifier checks generated Lean proofs against trusted challenge statements. With GPT-5.6 Sol, HOA achieves perfect scores on the IMO, IPhO, and IOI 2026, near-full marks on the IChO 2026 problem set, and solves all 672 problems (100%) on PutnamBench. | **[H2Table: Hierarchical Hypergraph-Enhanced Large Language Models for Complex Table Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01216v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tables are ubiquitous across diverse domains, yet reasoning over them remains a significant challenge for modern large language models (LLMs). Furthermore, we introduce a set of learnable query vectors acting as a lightweight bridge to extract representative structural embeddings from the encoder into the LLM. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach effectively handles complex table question answering tasks with hierarchical nested headers. Notably, on the HiTab dataset, H2Table achieves an average improvement of 22.88% over state-of-the-art baselines on highly complex tables with a nesting depth of four. Our code is available at: https://github.com/lila120/h2table.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 (Main Conference). Camera-ready version</p></details> |
| **[AMLworld-Compact: Importance-Weighted Downsampling for Cost-Effective LLM Evaluation and Error Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13627v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating large language models (LLMs) on AMLworld's smallest test splits is costly because each of over a million targets requires a separate graph prompt. Under review</p></details> |
| **[SWE-DeceptionBench: Benchmarking Deceptive Behaviors of LLMs Across the Software Development Lifecycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13626v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used throughout the software development lifecycle. These findings highlight the need for more effective deception detection and mitigation methods to support trustworthy LLM-based software engineering.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bolzano: From Expert-Guided Proof Search to Automated Open-Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09769v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly contributing to mathematical research, where progress often depends on efficient proof search, incremental improvements and careful verification. | **[Rationally Linearized MLPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13568v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present Rationally Linearized Multilayer Perceptrons(RaLiMPs), a new approach to efficiently linearizing MLPs using low-degree rational approximations of standard activation functions. | **[Forgetting is Not Always Bad: A Neuro-Inspired Memory Repair Mechanism for Poisoned LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13267v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are highly vulnerable to memory-poisoning attacks. | **[Human and LLM Facilitation in Collaborative Design Meetings: A Computational Behavioral Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13082v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored as facilitators of group discussion and collaborative work, but the behavioral differences between LLM-generated and human facilitation remain insufficiently characterized. The sampled human Project Manager turns were higher on contextual semantic distance (r = 0.644) and on empathyand-hedge lexical density (r = 0.203). | **[What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. One possible interpretation is that the natural-language responses made the AI advice easier to accept, which may have reduced participants' tendency to verify the underlying evidence when the IDS was incorrect. | **[Surfing the Activation Wave: Analyzing the Information and Geometry of LLM Hidden States for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12939v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Decoder hidden states from different layers of large language models (LLMs) often encode complementary information. | **[What Current AI Benchmarks Leave Unmeasured: Modality, Search, Citations, and Implications (for Safety Evaluations)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06202v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) benchmark evaluations are routinely used to support claims about model safety, reliability, and deployment readiness. | **[Hide and Seek: HIDE Concepts to SEEK explanations for black box models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13935v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interpreting machine learning model predictions is a central challenge, particularly for black-box vision models. Concept-based attribution methods interpret models using human-understandable concepts. Existing concept-based methods can only interpret classification and regression models. They require access to model weights or large concept-annotated datasets, and most methods cannot provide image-level attribution. We propose Hide and Seek (HnS), a new method for concept-level attribution that does not require access to model weights or labeled concept datasets. HnS can interpret a wide range of vision models. Given a concept and image set, HnS retrieves concept-aligned images using a vision-language model, localizes the concept within each image, masks the localized region, and measures attribution from the change in the black-box model's output between the original and masked images. This enables faithful attribution by directly measuring the model's behavior and supporting local attributions. We validated HnS on CelebA and COCO-Stuff. Compared to existing methods, HnS showed better agreement with ground-truth attributions. We demonstrated the applicability of HnS on diverse tasks, including image captioning and satellite image segmentation, where no existing concept attribution method can be used. We provided our anonymous code in the supplementary material.</p></details> |  |
| **[When VLMs Destroy What They Know: Spatial Representations Fall Below Lexical Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13910v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) increasingly analyze and reason about images, yet it remains unclear whether their internal representations faithfully encode the structured relations required for such reasoning. Across multiple VLM architectures, we find that spatial relations are represented substantially less faithfully than lexical information, even when the same models score well above chance on compositional benchmarks. Further analysis shows that the relation is identifiable at the predicate's own tokens but is lost when the sentence is aggregated into a global representation, whether through the (EOS) token or by mean pooling. These findings reveal a systematic failure invisible to output-based evaluation: VLMs do not preserve, in the representation used downstream, relational information that is present in their own input.</p></details> |  |

Given the initial observation and a textual description of the reconstruction objective, a vision-language model, guided by designed instructions, interprets the scene and constructs structured prompts for a video diffusion model. The resulting video sequence provides plausible future observations of the environment, which are subsequently processed using monocular ORB-SLAM3 for camera pose estimation and Video Depth Anything for dense depth prediction. Our central contribution is a geometry-guided depth alignment stage that combines the estimated camera poses with triangulated visual landmarks to constrain dense depth predictions across multiple views. This alignment improves the geometric consistency of the reconstructed 3D environment, addressing inconsistencies that temporal coherence of the generated video alone cannot resolve. The experimental evaluation of navigation in imagined 3D environment shows that across a 19-scene horizon study, planning-feasible scenes increase from 13/19 at P20 to 15/19 at P81, while successful RRT trials increase from 62/90 to 75/90. In a separate corridor study, 6 of 25 evaluable generated-camera trajectories violate the reconstructed collision constraints; RRT planner recovers feasible alternatives in 5 of those 6 cases. A larger archive contains 58 usable reconstructions from 67 attempted cases, of which 36 support a downstream plan and 22 do not, exposing reconstruction and free-space failure modes rather than hiding them without lidar. Submitted to IEEE ICRA 2027</p></details> |

Lastly, we demonstrate the utility of our scoring method for rejection sampling of generated programs for fine-tuning, improving Qwen3.5-9B by 16% and Phi-4-MM by 28% compared to their base checkpoints. | **[Studying Comics at Scale with Artificial Intelligence: The PAGE Framework and Three Studies of American Comics and Japanese Manga](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13840v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Comics are one of the great visual records of the twentieth century: hundreds of thousands of pages in which a mass readership saw its leaders, its wars and its heroes. | **[Improving Semantic Coverage in AI Test & Evaluation through Generative Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13697v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In test and evaluation (T&E) of AI models, evaluators typically have access only to a held-out test dataset and no knowledge of the training data or its semantic composition. If critical input conditions---such as time-of-day, weather, or scene location---are underrepresented in this test dataset, T&E results can be misleadingly due to this semantic coverage gap. We propose a three-stage generative AI pipeline to detect and fill such semantic gaps without requiring ground-truth labels of these semantics. First, vision-language models (VLMs) perform zero-shot binary labeling of semantic factors, and an embedding-space clustering step identifies which semantics most affect downstream model behavior. Second, diffusion-based image-to-image models transform existing covered images into the underrepresented semantic domain, preserving scene content while augmenting high-level appearance. Third, statistical analysis of the augmented image embeddings verifies that the synthetic imagery improves dataset coverage before it is incorporated. Crucially, the synthetic data is used only to enrich the test set and no model retraining is performed for T&E. We validate the full pipeline on both image classification and object detection under day/night semantic gaps, showing that the augmented test datasets better distinguish models with and without exposure to the missing semantic, thereby yielding T&E conclusions that more closely match those of an ideal, fully covered dataset. Code available at https://github.com/Hashe037/DEDUCE</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages total, previously published at Society of Photo-Optical Instrumentation Engineers (SPIE) Conference Series, Volume 14046</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Answer-Grounding Consistency in Remote Sensing Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13636v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In grounded remote sensing visual question answering, a model produces a textual answer and identifies relevant objects in the image as grounding evidence. We propose the Answer-Evidence Support Protocol (AESP) to assess this consistency through evidence support: whether the grounding evidence supports the model's own answer. AESP applies task-specific rules to existence, counting, and comparison questions. We further construct the Grounded Remote Sensing Visual Question Answering (G-RSVQA) benchmark, containing 15,027 samples with questions, reference answers, and all related object-level annotations. Experiments on seven grounded vision--language models reveal failures of evidence support across all three question types. Up to 50.82% of correct answers in the evidence evaluation subset are not supported by the provided grounding evidence.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under review at ICASSP 2027. Code available at https://github.com/pingggg516/AESP-Evaluation</p></details> |
| **[Auditing VLM Benchmarks from Existing Evaluation Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13548v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every evaluation run of a vision-language model (VLM) already produces per-question artifacts (predictions, judged correctness, metadata) that leaderboards reduce to a single score and then discard. We reuse those artifacts to audit the benchmark itself, and add an audit layer to VLMEvalKit: six detectors that run as post-processing over saved outputs, plus one additional text-only rerun with visual inputs removed, emitting a per-question report. The layer reports per-question findings together with aggregate audit statistics; for the headline quantities, it attaches permutation-null excess where applicable, interval estimates for proportions, and sensitivity to the model pool. Auditing MMStar, a benchmark curated specifically so that the image is indispensable, with three models, we find zero aggregate accuracy gain from the image on 20.2% of its questions. An item-matched permutation null accounts for 17.0%, leaving a +3.2 pp excess (corrected permutation p = 0.001). This excess is concentrated in the mathematics and science categories and is absent from coarse perception, a localization consistent with knowledge substituting for visual evidence; confirming that mechanism would require item-level review. A second detector flags 134 questions on which every model that answered in parseable form picked the same non-gold option, and these candidates peak in the perception categories, whereas the zero-gain excess is largest in mathematics and science & technology. Replacing the LLM judge with exact string matching changes no conclusion, and every scoring discrepancy between the two traces to one model whose free-form answers a parser cannot read. On a binary yes/no benchmark the detectors measurably fall out of calibration, a scope boundary that we measure and report. The audit layer, the prediction artifacts and the generated reports accompany this submission.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 5 figures. Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop TAE (Trust-AI-Eval): Can We Trust AI Evaluation? (non-archival). Code and artifacts: https://github.com/avos-rnd/vlm-bench</p></details> |
| **[Decoupling the Declarative from the Procedural in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21496v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying generalist robotic agents in the real world requires transferable skills. While these policies achieve state-of-the-art manipulation performance in-distribution, they remain brittle to minor spatial, semantic, and task variations. In this work, we address the inability of current models to decouple the declarative (i.e., concepts and entity semantics) from the procedural knowledge (i.e., how to do something) encoded in their parameters, which is a fundamental bottleneck for zero-shot skill transfer to novel objects. To address this, we propose w$^{2}$VLA, a new VLA model with restructured information flow. Rather than feeding all multimodal tokens from the VLM encoder into a large, opaque transformer-based action expert, our approach modulates the robot state sequence with visual, spatial, and skill information in a compositional and interpretable manner. Unlike popular, state-of-the-art VLAs, we show that our modular approach successfully decouples knowledge representations, enabling robust behavior cloning and unprecedented zero-shot skill transfer capabilities across dissimilar, unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgroGround: Multi-Granularity Grounded Recognition in Agriculture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04425v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agricultural visual models are typically evaluated for either recognition or localization, but reliable diagnosis requires identifying what is present and localizing the evidence. | **[LensVLM: Selective Context Expansion for Compressed Visual Representation of Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07019v4)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision Language Models (VLMs) offer the exciting possibility of processing text as rendered images, bypassing the need for tokenizing the text into long token sequences. Since VLM image encoders map fixed-size images to a fixed number of visual tokens, varying rendering resolution provides a fine-grained compression knob. To address this, we propose LensVLM, an inference framework and post-training recipe that enables VLMs to scan compressed images, then selectively expand only the relevant images to their uncompressed form via learned tools. Building on Qwen3.5-9B-Base, LensVLM maintains accuracy comparable to the full-text upper bound at 4.3$\times$ effective compression and outperforms retrieval-based, text- and visual-compression baselines up to 10.1$\times$ effective compression across seven text QA benchmarks. LensVLM also generalizes to multimodal document and code understanding tasks, with the accuracy gain over baselines growing as compression increases. Our analysis validates this approach: training makes visual compression robust to rendering choices, and as compression grows the model increasingly relies on expanded content rather than unreliable visual reading. The analysis also yields practical tool-choice guidance: text expansion is preferable for rendered text, while high-resolution image expansion suits native documents whose layout cues carry task-relevant information.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[Beyond a Single Crop: Contextualized Embeddings for Player Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13259v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Person re-identification is typically addressed by learning identity-discriminative embeddings from individual person crops. Here, we propose ContextReID, a contextualized embedding framework that represents a player by jointly considering other players in the same frame and observations from different frames of the broadcast sequence. By incorporating both co-occurring players and cross-frame observations, the model learns identity features relative to the surrounding visual context rather than relying only on isolated appearance cues. | **[Forward Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13117v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models and world action models (WAMs) have advanced robotic policy learning by building on pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) and video generative models (VGMs), respectively. We present Forward Dynamics Models (FDM), a family of robotic action models that couples action and future world-state prediction through an action-to-future paradigm. Future prediction is conditioned on action features, never the reverse: end-to-end training lets ground-truth future states supervise the policy through these features, while prediction errors cannot propagate into actions. At inference, FDM skips future generation entirely, avoiding its computational cost. The paradigm is backbone-agnostic, and we instantiate it with both VLM and VGM backbones. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate the effectiveness of FDM across both backbone families, achieving a state-of-the-art 99.4% average success rate on LIBERO and absolute gains of up to 13% over VLA and WAM methods on real-world tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://logosroboticsgroup.github.io/FDM/</p></details> |
| **[What Do Visual-Head Discovery Scores Actually Find? Attention Source, Necessity, and Random-Control Pitfalls in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13111v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Attention heads that attend to image regions relevant to a question are increasingly used to steer, ground and interpret vision-language models (VLMs) and to reduce their hallucinations. They are mostly found by ranking heads with an attention- pattern score and validated by steering or masking them. We ask how the choices inside such a discovery score shape the heads it returns, and which of the two validations identifies them. On Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct we vary the query type, the discovery corpus and the token positions whose attention is read (the attention source) in a full 2 x 3 x 2 design, and test all 12 head-sets for steerability (CERS: Contrastive Evidence-Region Steering score) and necessity (NCS: Necessity Con- trast Score) against layer-matched random head-sets. (1) The attention source and the query type reshape the selected heads far more than the corpus: changing the source keeps a median of 49 of the top-100 heads, the query 55 and the cor- pus 84, while two halves of the same data share 96-99. (2) Necessity follows the attention source: head-sets discovered from query-span attention are more nec- essary than last-token ones (difference 0.083, p=6 x 10^-10), and the two re- ferring + query-span sets lie 17-19 standard deviations above 250 layer-matched random sets (Holm-adjusted p=0.048). The referring + query-span effect re- plicates on several recent VLMs. (3) Steerability is not specific to the discovered heads: no discovered head-set steers better than its layer-matched random sets (rank p >= 0.14), and random sets drawn from the same or from any layer span the discovered sets' scores. Necessity therefore separates the discovery settings, while steering at this strength does not. We turn these findings into a protocol for reporting visual-head discoveries.</p></details> |  |
| **[GeoGR: Spatial-Semantic Token Compression and Group Routing for Efficient 3D VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13035v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-view representations enable pretrained image and video vision-language models to understand 3D scenes, but their long visual token sequences make inference costly. Unselected voxel features are aggregated into contextual anchors to retain scene information that would otherwise be discarded. At the LLM level, we identify an effective visual computation window and observe that query-conditioned token relevance remains locally stable but changes over longer layer intervals. Guided by these findings, GroupRoute combines late entry and early exit with recoverable group-wise skipping. Low-relevance tokens bypass complete LLM layers while retaining their hidden states and can rejoin the active group at subsequent anchor layers selected under a computation budget. For compression-aware post-training, we construct a compact dataset of 67K samples by retaining 30% of the original training pool. With this protocol, GeoGR retains 95.2% and 96.8% of the dense post-trained models' performance on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Video-3D LLM-7B, respectively, while accelerating LLM prefill by $6.8\times$ and $6.1\times$. | **[MVGraph: Benchmarking LVLMs on Evidence Composition across Multi-View Visual Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12961v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce MVGraph, a benchmark for evaluating large vision-language models (LVLMs) on multi-graph reasoning where the evidence required by a query is distributed across multiple interrelated visual graph views. | **[In-Context Learning Can Help Vision-Language Models Overcome Training Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12942v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) learn strong statistical regularities during training, which can make them fail to properly respond to visual evidence when input images violate those regularities. Under globally Lipschitz gradients, robust LMC (R-LMC) handles the proposed oracle through geometric-median centering and residual clipping. With exact gradients, adaptive LMC (A-LMC) controls discretization error under generalized smoothness. Robust adaptive LMC (RA-LMC) addresses both relaxations through robust estimation and a noise-aware step size. | **[NovaPlan: Zero-Shot Long-Horizon Manipulation via Closed-Loop Video Language Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20119v2)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Solving complex long-horizon robotic tasks requires joint reasoning over abstract task structure and low-level physical interaction. | **[$Δ$Representation: Geometry Supervised Representation Learning of Phenotypes via Counterfactual Reasoning for Medical VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10286v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical vision-language models (VLMs) have shown increasing potential for radiological image interpretation. | **[EasyLens: A Training-Free Plug-and-Play Subtle-Lesion Representation Amplifier for Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06379v4)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical vision-language models (VLMs) have shown increasing potential for clinical image interpretation, including lesion detection and report generation. | **[Towards Physically Grounded Articulated 3D Assets: A Multi-Agent Pipeline and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13947v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we study physically grounded articulated 3D asset generation---moving the goal from reproducing how objects look to capturing how they move and function. Furthermore, we construct a unified benchmark spanning four key aspects: spatial fidelity, physical plausibility, downstream task performance, and overall quality. The benchmark encompasses 100 reference assets from ArtVIP and LightWheel, 50 stability prompts, and 50 ABO objects with reference masses, with protocols evaluating various metrics. Through extensive evaluation of more than 10 representative models, Artisan achieves competitive spatial fidelity while attaining the lowest collision rates and highest stability rates among the evaluated methods. Together, our system and benchmark establish insights for advancing 3D generation towards physically plausible, functionally usable assets.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Harness-Induced Belief Divergence in Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software agents are becoming increasingly important in modern coding workflows, where they solve complex tasks through multi-step interaction with code repositories. A central component of these systems is the execution harness, which governs how the underlying LLM observes, acts on, and verifies its environment. We show that the harness can in turn shape the agent's evolving beliefs about the environment, even when the task, environment, and base LLM remain fixed. To reveal this effect, we introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how an agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under alternative harnesses. We further separate immediate divergence introduced by the interface from divergence that develops over longer horizons. Across controlled coding tasks include HIBench-Code, SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench, we find substantial cross-harness belief divergence that is often invisible from terminal success alone and can propagate into different downstream actions. Building on this observation, we introduce \textbf{Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM)}, which exposes evidence hidden or compressed by harness execution and aligns the resulting belief trajectories across different harnesses. Our findings reveal that harness design can systematically shape how software agents understand and act on their environments, providing practical guidance for building and evaluating more reliable coding agents.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[RATED: Reliability-Aware AI Agent Tool Retrieval via Execution Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13889v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly rely on external tools, accurate retrieval is essential for reliable behavior. RATED employs a frozen dual-encoder with contrastive fine-tuning for semantic matching, and a lightweight contextual bandit module that re-ranks candidates based on real-time execution feedback. Recipes sampled from this graph are instantiated as realistic scenarios in which agents can produce harmful outcomes. We further introduce a reinforcement learning framework that improves length extrapolation and adaptively determines the number of rereading passes according to task complexity, enabling flexible control of computational overhead. Our MemReread-4B outperforms strong baselines on long-context reasoning tasks (e.g., +1.6pp v.s. DeepSeek-V4-Pro on 2WikiMultiHopQA) while maintaining linear time complexity with respect to context length.</p></details> |  |
| **[SEEDS: Preview-Guided Evidence Admission for Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12932v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Search agents extend large reasoning models with multi-round access to external knowledge, yet most existing systems largely conflate retrieving evidence with consuming it. Retrieved documents are commonly incorporated according to fixed or rank-based policies, despite the fact that evidence utility is conditioned on the agent's current reasoning state. Consequently, a lower-ranked candidate may be critical for the current step, while a higher-ranked candidate may provide redundant, irrelevant, or misleading information. We formulate this overlooked problem as evidence admission, which asks, at each search step, which retrieved candidates should be allowed to enter the agent's evolving reasoning context. To enable such selective control, we introduce SEEDS (Selective Evidence Entry During Search), a preview-guided evidence admission framework for search agents. SEEDS first exposes lightweight previews of retrieved candidates and then lets the agent jointly determine which candidates and how many should be fully inspected, conditioned on its current reasoning state. Unlike fixed or adaptive prefix policies, SEEDS can select arbitrary subsets with flexible cardinality, and it can be instantiated directly with an existing reasoning agent or further optimized through learning. Across nine knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks under both local corpus and live web search, SEEDS consistently improves search effectiveness while substantially reducing full-evidence consumption. Experimental analyses show that SEEDS more effectively selects useful candidates beyond the corresponding retrieval prefix. These results establish evidence admission as a complementary dimension of agentic search, separating what information is retrieved from what information is ultimately allowed to shape reasoning.</p></details> | 7 figures, 20 pages |
| **[Network Topology Shapes AI Intervention Effectiveness on Deception: Truth-Telling vs. Investigative Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12931v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying AI agents to combat dishonest communication is an intuitive response to deception in hybrid human-AI societies. However, the efficacy of such interventions in realistic networked settings remains unclear. We study this problem using an evolutionary game-theoretic model of the Deceiver's Game by extending it to structured networks, where agents can adopt deception, truth-telling, and investigation strategies. We introduce committed AI agents (zealots) fixed to either truth-telling or investigative strategies and conduct agent-based simulations across three network topologies. Our key findings are: (1) passive committed truth-telling agents can amplify adaptive deception by creating exploitable honest partners, whereas investigative agents increase adaptive truth-telling in the oscillatory regime; (2) intervention effects are non-monotonic in deployment density because committed agents reshape rather than simply suppress the underlying oscillatory dynamics; and (3) response peaks depend on topology, with sampled scale-free populations showing sharp low-density responses and fully connected populations requiring larger committed fractions. Overall, the results highlight the need to distinguish passive truth-telling from active investigation when using committed agents to influence networked deception dynamics.</p></details> |  |
| **[DREAM: Dynamic Resolution Assignment For Multimodal Multi-agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05615v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent debate (MAD) has emerged as an effective paradigm to improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) and is increasingly being extended to multimodal settings. However, existing multimodal MAD frameworks typically expose agents to the same fixed visual input, ignoring substantial variation in the visual scale needed across samples and agents. In addition, these frameworks frequently suffer from groupthink, a phenomenon where agents prematurely abandon correct deductions to conform with confident but hallucinated peer responses. To address these bottlenecks, we introduce DREAM (Dynamic Resolution Assignment For Multimodal Multi-Agent Debate), which operates via two core components: (1) Dynamic Resolution Assignment, a zero-shot probe round where agents test multiple resolutions, quantify uncertainty using Average Normalized Log-Likelihood (ANLL), and use an adaptive threshold to assign each agent to its empirically optimal resolution; (2) Uncertainty-Guided Rollback Aggregation counters groupthink by tracking each agent's uncertainty over rounds and restoring early low-uncertainty answers overridden by group pressure. On six multimodal datasets, DREAM improves the accuracy-token trade-off over multi-agent debate baselines by 1.5-3.2% accuracy without dataset-specific tuning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with a Physics-Informed World Model for Cooperative Mixed Traffic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17739v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study investigates cooperative control of connected and automated vehicles (CAVs) at partially observable highway bottlenecks in mixed traffic, aiming to mitigate congestion without relying on complete global traffic states or online trial-and-error. We propose a physics-informed world model-based offline multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that reconstructs a physically interpretable global traffic state from local CAV observation-action histories, with coupled macroscopic-microscopic traffic dynamics providing physics-based supervision. A probabilistic ensemble world model learns traffic-state transitions and system rewards, while model disagreement quantifies epistemic uncertainty. Multi-step imagined rollouts with pessimistic rewards and uncertainty-driven truncation are then used for offline policy learning. Experiments in a SUMO-based on-ramp bottleneck using approximately $1\times10^6$ offline transitions show that physics supervision improves state reconstruction and world-model prediction accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[SWE-Bench Pro Verified: A Reliable Benchmark for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08149v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>SWE-Bench Pro has emerged as a standard benchmark for evaluating software engineering agents on challenging repository-level tasks. However, our analysis work show that its evaluation is undermined by two sources of unreliability: reward hacking, enabled by leakage of gold solutions or hidden evaluation information, and task quality issues, including misleading problem statements and improperly scoped tests. These issues can inflate benchmark performance and obscure agents' true coding ability. We present SWE-Bench Pro Verified, a verified version of SWE-Bench Pro that addresses both problems. Our approach combines anti-hacking safeguards that eliminate major leakage channels without disrupting normal agent functionality, with task refinement that minimally corrects inconsistencies within flawed instances. Evaluations on SWE-Bench Pro Verified reveal that some models perform substantially worse than previously evaluated, suggesting that existing results on SWE-Bench Pro may overestimate real software engineering capability. SWE-Bench Pro Verified offers a more trustworthy benchmark for assessing software engineering agents.</p></details> |  |
